

===== PDF PAGE 1 =====

SOERGEL CALCULUS FOR MONODROMIC HECKE CATEGORIES
COLTON SANDVIK
Abstract. We introduce two 2-categories which categorify the monodromic Hecke algebra. The
first is algebraic in nature and generalizes Abe’s theory of Soergel bimodules.
The second is a
diagrammatic category defined via generators and relations which generalizes the Elias–Williamson
diagrammatic calculus. As our first main result, we prove that these algebraic and diagrammatic
categorifications are equivalent, extending an earlier theorem of Abe. Furthermore, we relate these
new categorifications to a third categorification via parity sheaves which was previously studied by
the author. More precisely, we provide a monodromic analogue of a theorem of Riche and Williamson
to show that the diagrammatic category is equivalent to the monodromic Hecke category of parity
sheaves associated to a reductive group. Finally, we show that these monodromic Hecke categories
can be described by unipotent Hecke categories associated to endoscopic Coxeter groups.

1. Introduction
1.1. Hecke Algebras. Let G be a connected reductive group over F = Fp. Pick a prime ℓ̸= p and a
power q = pn. Let B ⊂G be a Borel subgroup with split maximal torus T ⊂B. The Hecke algebra
H(Fq) := FunB(Fq)×B(Fq)(G(Fq), Qℓ)
is the algebra of B(Fq)-biinvariant Qℓ-valued functions on G(Fq) with multiplication given by convo-
lution. The Hecke algebra was originally introduced to study unipotent Qℓ-representations of G(Fq);
however, since their introduction, Hecke algebras have become ubiquitous throughout many areas of
representation theory.
In the study of the non-unipotent representation theory of G(Fq), Lusztig [Lus19] introduced a
generalization of the Hecke algebra which is now called the monodromic Hecke algebra. Let χ, χ′ :
T(Fq) →Q
×
ℓbe two characters of the maximal torus. We can regard χ and χ′ as characters B(Fq) →
Q
×
ℓby the projection B →T. We can then consider the Qℓ-vector space of (B(Fq) × B(Fq), χ × χ′)-
invariant Qℓ-valued functions on G(Fq),
χHmon
χ′
(Fq) := Fun(B(Fq)×B(Fq),χ×χ′)(G(Fq), Qℓ)
= {f : G(Fq) →Q
×
ℓ| f(b1gb2) = χ(b1)f(g)χ′(b2) for b1, b2 ∈B, g ∈G}.

When χ = χ′, χHmon

(Fq) is also an algebra under convolution. When χ ̸= χ′, χHmon

(Fq) is no longer
an algebra. Instead, convolution gives suitably associative maps
χHmon

If we fix a W-orbit o of characters T(Fq) →Qℓ, we can define the monodromic Hecke algebra as the
direct sum of these vector spaces
Hmon(Fq) =

which becomes an algebra under convolution and the relation χHmon

Iwahori gave a presentation of the Hecke algebra which is independent of Fq and only depends on the
Weyl group W [Iwa64]. In particular, Iwahori defined a Z[v±]-algebra H such that H(Fq) ∼= H⊗Z[v±]Qℓ
along the specialization v−1 7→√q ∈Qℓ. This presentation allowed for the generalization of the Hecke
algebra to any Coxeter group and not just the crystallographic ones. We write H(W) for the (non-
specialized) Hecke algebra with Coxeter group W.
Lusztig has given a presentation of the monodromic Hecke algebra closely inspired by Iwahori’s pre-
sentation [Lus16]. In particular, Lusztig defined a Z[v±]-algebra Hmon(o) which specializes to Hmon(Fq)
along v−1 7→√q. Note that this presentation still depends on q since o depends on q; however, this
presentation is still favorable for the purposes of categorification. To remove the restriction to a fixed
W-orbit o of characters, we instead define the monodromic Hecke algebroid. Instead of an algebra,
the monodromic Hecke algebroid Hmon(Fq) is a category whose objects are characters T(Fq) →Q
×
ℓ
and whose morphisms are the vector spaces χHmon
χ′
(Fq). The composition in Hmon(Fq) is given by
convolution. We give a presentation of this algebroid in §3.3 by means of generators and relations.
This presentation allows us to define the monodromic Hecke algebroid over an arbitrary Coxeter group
W and allows us to replace the set of characters T(Fq) →Q
×
ℓby any W-set o.
Lusztig has shown that the monodromic Hecke algebra is closely related to the ordinary Hecke
algebra [Lus19]. For simplicity, assume that G has connected center. For each character χ of T(Fq),
one can associate a reductive group H◦
χ, called the endoscopic group. We denote its Weyl group by
W ◦
χ. In loc. cit., it is then proved that there is an isomorphism of algebras
χHmon
χ
(Fq, W) ∼= H(Fq, W ◦
χ).
This isomorphism is called the monodromic-endoscopic equivalence. Moreover, a similar description of
all of Hmon can be given in terms of ordinary Hecke algebras for the Weyl groups of endoscopic groups.
This is closely related with Lusztig’s classification of G(Fq)-representations where the non-unipotent
representations are parameterized by unipotent representations of the endoscopic groups.
1.2. Motivation from Geometry. In their foundational work [KL79], Kazhdan and Lusztig cate-
gorified the Hecke algebra H via the sheaf-function correspondence. In particular, one can define the
geometric Hecke category as the category Semis(B\G/B, Qℓ) of semisimple étale constructible sheaves
on the stack B\G/B. The geometric Hecke category is a monoidal category under convolution. The
split Grothendieck group [Semis(B\G/B, Qℓ)]⊕is then an algebra. In loc. cit., it is then shown that
there is an isomorphism of algebras
[Semis(B\G/B, Qℓ)]⊕∼= H.
Moreover, this isomorphism takes the simple IC-sheaves to the Kazhdan–Lusztig basis.
The geometric Hecke category is closely related with the category of unipotent Qℓ-representations
of finite groups of Lie type (cf., [BZN09,BFO12,Lus05]). This poses the question: if one replaces Qℓ-
coefficients with Fℓ-coefficients, does the geometric Hecke category give information about unipotent
Fℓ-representations? The answer is yes, but with some degree of caution. Namely, with Fℓ-coefficients,
the decomposition theorem fails and the category of semisimple complexes is no longer the correct
object of study. There are two common replacements in the literature:


===== PDF PAGE 4 =====

4
COLTON SANDVIK
(1) One can instead work with the derived category of constructible sheaves on B\G/B instead of
just the semisimple complexes. This category has many nice formal properties and in [Zhu16]
it is shown that its categorical center is closely related with unipotent Fℓ-representations of
finite groups of Lie type. However, the category is hard to compute with. Moreover, it no
longer categorifies H, but instead just the group algebra Z[W].
(2) Another option is to work with the category of parity sheaves Par(B\G/B, Fℓ) introduced
in [JMW14].
While it is less clear what applications this category has to representations
of finite groups of Lie type, it does provide a novel categorification of H.
Moreover, the
indecomposable parity sheaves get sent to the “p-Kazhdan–Lusztig basis” which has found
numerous applications throughout modular representation theory.
One can ask how much of this story transfers over to the monodromic setting. In [LY20], Lusztig
and Yun gave a geometric categorification of the monodromic Hecke algebra. In this setup, a character
χ : T(Fq) →Q
×
ℓ
get associated to a character sheaf Lχ ∈Loc(T, Qℓ).
One can then define the
geometric (Lχ, Lχ′)-monodromic Hecke category as the category
Semis(T ×T,Lχ⊠L−1
χ′ )(U\G/U, Qℓ)
of (T × T, Lχ ⊠L−1
χ′ )-equivariant semisimple complexes on the stack U\G/U.
This is a monoidal
category under convolution of sheaves.
For simplicity, we again assume that G has connected center. The main result of loc. cit. upgrades
the description of the monodromic-endoscopic equivalence of Hecke algebras to Hecke categories. Note
that H◦
χ is defined so that T is a maximal torus for H◦
χ. A Borel subgroup B of G containing T
determines a Borel subgroup Bχ of H◦
χ containing T as well. Lusztig and Yun [LY20] proved that
there is an equivalence of monoidal categories
Semis(T ×T,Lχ⊠Lχ)(U\G/U, Qℓ) ∼= Semis(Bχ\H◦
χ/Bχ, Qℓ).
This equivalence can also be extended to the case of unequal parameters χ, χ′ and G having a discon-
nected center.
In [San25], we extended the result of [LY20] to positive characteristic coefficients.
We defined
categories of parity sheaves
Par(T ×T,Lχ⊠L−1
χ′ )(U\G/U, Fℓ).
We then showed that there is an equivalence of monoidal categories
Par(T ×T,Lχ⊠L−1
χ )(U\G/U, Fℓ) ∼= Par(Bχ\H◦
χ/Bχ, Fℓ).
These categories of parity sheaves can be assembled into a 2-category denoted by
\Par/(G, Fℓ)
We further showed in [San25, Theorem 3.8.4] that the split Grothendieck algebroid of
\Par/(G, Fℓ)
is isomorphic to the monodromic Hecke algebroid. More generally, the Fℓ-coefficients appearing here
can be replaced by k-coefficients where k is a field or a complete local ring.
1.3. Soergel Bimodules. In [Soe90,Soe92,Soe00], Soergel constructed another categorification of the
Hecke algebra using bimodules over the equivariant cohomology ring
R = H•
T (pt; C) ∼= Sym(h∗)
where h is the Lie algebra of T. Note that R is a graded algebra over k with h∗in degree 2. The
Weyl group W acts on R, and from this, one considers the subalgebra of s-invariants, denoted Rs, for
s ∈S. Soergel bimodules are then generated by taking direct sums and direct summands from graded
R-bimodules of the form
R ⊗Rs1 R ⊗Rs2 . . . ⊗Rsk R(n)
where s1, . . . , sk ∈S and (n) denotes a shift in grading by n ∈Z. The resulting category of Soergel
bimodules, denoted SBim(h, W), again categorifies the Hecke algebra.


===== PDF PAGE 5 =====

SOERGEL CALCULUS FOR MONODROMIC HECKE CATEGORIES
5
The key advantage with using Soergel bimodules is that they can be easily generalized to non-
crystallographic Coxeter groups. In [Soe07], Soergel extends the eponymous theory to arbitrary Coxeter
systems W and “reflection faithful” representations h of W. This affords a categorification of Hecke
algebras for non-crystallographic Coxeter groups.
Unfortunately, Soergel bimodules starts to become ill-behaved whenever W is infinite or k has small
characteristic (in which case, reflection faithful representations are hard to find). More recently, Abe
[Abe21] was able to rectify this undesirable behavior by enhancing the category of Soergel bimodules.
Roughly speaking, in Abe’s incarnation of Soergel bimodules, one also keeps track of the various
localization data associated to a given bimodule.
This localization data helps to shrink the Hom
spaces and ensures that Soergel bimodules associated to distinct elements of W stay distinct in the
Grothendieck group. As a consequence, Abe’s enhancement of Soergel bimodules, denoted A(h, W),
again categorifies the Hecke algebra.
Building off of work in [LY20], we will develop an analogous theory for the monodromic Hecke
algebroid associated to a Coxeter group W with a W-set o and a W-realization h. In the monodromic
theory, one considers two types of building blocks for Soergel bimodules associated to a simple reflection
s ∈S. The first is the non-monodromic Soergel bimodule Cs := R ⊗Rs R(1) associated to s which we
use whenever s stabilizes the monodromy parameter L. The second is the s-twisted standard bimodule
Rs which we use whenever s does not stabilize L. The two different types of building blocks can then be
used together to produce a 2-category of monodromic Soergel bimodules over o, denoted \A/(h, W, o).
Monodromic Soergel bimodules have a convenient feature compared to parity sheaves. Since the
category of monodromic Soergel bimodules is a subcategory of R-bimodules, it is quite straightfor-
ward to prove a categorified version of the monodromic-endoscopic equivalence.
For L ∈o, write
L\A/L(h, W, o) for the endomorphism category of L viewed as an object of
\A/(h, W, o). There is
a decomposition of
L\A/L(h, W, o) into a direct sum of full subcategories called blocks. We write
L\A◦
/L(h, W, o) for the block containing the monoidal unit R.
There is a normal subgroup W ◦
L of
the stabilizer of L in W called the endoscopic Coxeter group. This recovers the Weyl group of the
endoscopic group when we are in the setting from before.
Proposition 1.3.1 (Proposition 3.5.7). There is an equivalence of monoidal categories
L\A◦
/L(h, W, o) ∼= A(h, W ◦
L).
This can also be extended to an equivalence of the 2-category
\A/(h, W, o) with a 2-category of
Abe–Soergel bimodules constructed by gluing the categories A(h, W ◦
L) together as L ∈o varies (see
Theorem 3.5.10).
1.4. Diagrammatic Hecke Category. There is one final categorification of the Hecke algebra we will
consider. Elias and Williamson asked, and answered, the following question: is it possible to present
the category of Soergel bimodules by means of generators and relations [EW16]? The morphisms of
the Elias–Williamson diagrammatic Hecke category D(h, W) are defined using certain isotopy classes
of decorated planar graphs. We will not review these generators and relations at this time, and instead
defer the construction to later on.
One key result from [EW16] is that the Grothendieck group of the diagrammatic Hecke category
is isomorphic to the Hecke algebra. The strategy for proving this result is rather involved. Namely,
they define a basis for the Hom spaces D(h, W) called the double leaves basis which is closely based
on Libedinsky’s light leaves basis [Lib08] for Soergel bimodules. The hard part is showing that a given
(potentially massive) diagram can be simplified via the defining relations into a linear combination of
double leaves.
We will perform an analogous construction in the monodromic setting. Namely, we will give a pre-
sentation of monodromic Soergel bimodules in terms of generators and relations. Since the categories
involved are 2-categories, the resulting generators turn out to be planar graphs with faces labeled by
monodromy parameters. We also construct a double leaves basis for the monodromic diagrammatic
Hecke category following ideas of [EW16].
The cellular structure of the double leaves basis along


===== PDF PAGE 6 =====

6
COLTON SANDVIK
with a mixed-sheaf formalism allows us to deduce that the monodromic diagrammatic Hecke category
categorifies the monodromic Hecke algebroid regardless of the base ring of the realization.
1.5. Main Results. We have at this point reviewed three different incarnations of the Hecke category.
Since they all have isomorphic Grothendieck groups (when they are all properly defined), one can ask
whether the categories themselves are equivalent. Two key theorems give answers in the affirmative.
First, Abe [Abe21] has shown that his incarnation of Soergel bimodules is equivalent to the Elias–
Williamson diagrammatic category. Abe’s result extends a similar result by [EW16] when h is reflection
faithful. Second, Riche and Williamson [RW18] have shown that the parity sheaf incarnation of the
Hecke category is equivalent to the Elias–Williamson diagrammatic category.
We are now ready to state our main results which are monodromic variants of the Abe and the
Riche–Williamson theorems.
Theorem 1.5.1 (Monodromic Abe). There is an equivalence of 2-categories
ΥA : \D/(h, W, o)
∼
→\A/(h, W, o).
Theorem 1.5.2 (Monodromic Riche–Williamson). Assume that k is either a finite extension of Zℓ,
the residue field of such an extension, or the field of fractions of such an extension. Let G be a connected
complex reductive group with Weyl group W and denote by h the Kac–Moody realization for G. Let
o be the W-orbit of a torsion character sheaf L on the maximal torus T. There is an equivalence of
2-categories
ΥRW : \D/(h, W, o)
∼
→\Par/(G, k).
Remark 1.5.3. These theorems along with the monodromic-endoscopic equivalence for Soergel bi-
modules gives rise to monodromic-endoscopic equivalences for the diagrammatic category.
Remark 1.5.4. One may hope to extend the monodromic Riche–Williamson theorem to arbitrary
Kac–Moody groups or for more general rings k. The only obstruction is in defining the image under
ΥRW of a simple reflection in s ∈S when Ls ̸= L. In this case, the minimal IC sheaf associated to L
and s is only well-defined up to a choice of scalar. It is hard to check whether these choices can be
made in a sufficiently coherent manner to ensure the functor is well-defined.
1.6. Organization. In §2, we will study systems of monodromy parameters for arbitrary Coxeter
groups. Associated to a monodromy parameter, we will construct the endoscopic Coxeter group. We
will further discuss realizations of Coxeter groups and the necessary 2-colored quantum combinatorics
needed to have well-behaved theories of monodromic Abe–Bott–Samelson bimodules.
In §3, we introduce the 2-category of monodromic Abe–Bott–Samelson bimodules. As part of their
study, we will establish an algebraic version of the monodromic-endoscopic equivalence. We continue
the study of the algebraic category in §4 where we develop a version of the double leaves basis for
monodromic Abe–Bott–Samelson bimodules.
In §5, we introduce the main character of the paper– the diagrammatic monodromic Hecke category.
We will then define a functor from the diagrammatic category to the algebraic category. Finally, we
will provide a diagrammatic incarnation of the double leaves basis, and deduce an equivalence between
the diagrammatic monodromic Hecke category and the category of Abe–Bott–Samelson bimodules
(Theorem 1.5.1). We delay the proof that double leaves span until §6.
In §7, we recall that geometric monodromic Hecke category of [San25]. We then construct a functor
from the geometric category to the diagrammatic category and show that this functor is an equivalence
(Theorem 1.5.2).
The paper includes two appendices. In §A, we prove that the functor from the diagrammatic Hecke
category to the category of Bott–Samelson bimodules is invariant under 3-color relations. This effort
is largely computational and depends on an exhaustive search of the possible systems of monodromy
parameters. In §B, we develop a general theory of “mixed derived categories” for object-adapted cellular
categories closely based on [ARV20].

2. Monodromic Coxeter Systems
2.1. Endoscopic Coxeter Group.
2.1.1. Notation. Let (W, S) be a Coxeter system and let e ∈W denote the identity. By definition W
is the group generated by the set of simple reflections S subject to the relations:
(2.1.1)
s2 = e for all s ∈S,
(2.1.2)
sts . . .
| {z }
ms,t
= tst . . .
| {z }
ms,t
for all s ̸= t ∈S.
The numbers ms,t ∈Z>0 ∪{∞} associated to each pair of distinct simple reflections determine W.
The group W is equipped with the Bruhat order ≤and the length function ℓ: W →Z≥0. If W is
finite, then it admits the longest element w0 under the Bruhat order. The rank of W is the cardinality
of S which we allow to be (countably) infinite.
For any subset I ⊂S, the corresponding parabolic subgroup WI of W is the subgroup generated
by s ∈I. It follows that (WI, I) is also Coxeter system.
We write Exp(W) for the set of expressions in W. These consist of sequences w = (s1, . . . , sk) of
simple reflections. We write w = (sk, . . . , s1) for the reversed expression of w. The length of w is
ℓ(w) := k. Note that ℓ(w) ≥ℓ(w) where w = s1 . . . sk is the product, and equality holds if and only
if w is a reduced expression. We adopt the convention of [EW16] and shorten “reduced expression” to
rex with the plural form being rexes.
A subexpression e of an expression w = (s1, . . . , sk) is a tuple (e1, . . . , ek) where ei ∈{0, 1} for all
i = 1, . . . , k. Given such a subexpression, we can consider the evaluation of w at e which is the element
we := se1
1 . . . sek
k ∈W. The Bruhat stroll associated to e is the sequence w0, . . . , wk in W defined by
wi = se1
1 . . . sei
i . We denote the set of all subexpressions of w by Sub(w).
Suppose we have a pair of distinct simple reflections s, t. For k ∈Z>1, we denote the expressions
given by alternating sequences starting with s (resp. t) by
sk := (s, t, s, . . .)
|
{z
}
k terms
and
tk := (t, s, t, . . .)
|
{z
}
k terms
.
We write sk and tk for the elements in W obtained by evaluating sk and tk respectively. We could
also consider alternating expressions ending in s or t, denoted ks = sk and kt = tk. Their evaluations
will similarly be denoted by ks and kt respectively.
A reflection in W is an element of the form wsw−1 for w ∈W and s ∈S. Every reflection is clearly
an involution. We denote the set of reflections in W by R(W).
2.1.2. Basic Definitions. Let (W, S) be a Coxeter system.
Definition 2.1.3. A set of monodromy parameters is a set o equipped with a right W-action on o.
Given monodromy parameters L, L′ ∈o, we define
LWL′ := {w ∈W | Lw = L′}.
If L = L′, we will often write WL := LWL.
From o, we can construct a groupoid M o(W) whose object set is o and whose set of morphisms
from L to L′ is given by LWL′. Composition in M o(W) is given by multiplication in W.
Example 2.1.4.
(1) Let o be a set with a single object. Then W acts trivially on o. In this case,
M o(W) is isomorphic as a groupoid to W. We often call this example the non-monodromic or
unipotent case. We will sometimes write o = 1 to refer to o having only one object which we
also denote by 1.


===== PDF PAGE 8 =====

8
COLTON SANDVIK
(2) Let o = W and have W act on o by right multiplication. In this case, M o(W) is equivalent as
a groupoid to the trivial group.
(3) The running example for a set of monodromy parameters comes from geometry. Namely, let G
be a connected reductive group (or more generally a Kac–Moody group) over C with maximal
torus T and Weyl group W. Let k be a commutative ring. We can then consider T ∨
k , the
Langland’s dual torus of T. In this case, o is the k-points of T ∨
k .
(4) Let I ⊂S and consider WI ⊆W the parabolic subgroup generated by I. We define o = WI\W
and have W act via right multiplication. Let e denote the identity coset in o. Then We = WI.
More generally, for x, y ∈WI\W, then xWy = xWIy−1.
(5) Example 2.1.4 (4) admits a mild generalization. Let W ′ ⊆W be a subgroup. Define o = W ′\W
and have W act via right multiplication. Let e denote the identity coset. Then We = W ′.
The set WL is a subgroup of W, but it need not be a parabolic subgroup. In fact, WL need not
admit a Coxeter structure whatsoever. However, we will show that there is a normal subgroup W ◦
L of
WL which (naturally) can be endowed with a Coxeter structure.
Note that Exp(W) is a monoid under concatenation with identity ∅– the empty expression. There
is an antiinvolution of Exp(W) taking w ∈Exp(W) to the reversed expression w. Moreover, there is a
map of monoids ev : Exp(W) →W given by evaluating an expression in W. If o is a set of monodromy
parameters for W, then there is a monoid action of Exp(W) on o via the evaluation map ev.
Let w = (s1, . . . , sk) ∈Exp(W) and L ∈o. For each 0 ≤i ≤k, we write w≤i for the expression
given by the first i terms of w. By definition w≤0 = ∅. We then define
K(w, L) := {1 ≤i ≤k | Lw≤i = Lw≤i−1}.

This procedure defines a map bL : Exp(W) →Exp(W) taking w to we. We also define bL := ev ◦bL.
Lemma 2.1.5. Let x, y ∈Exp(W) and L ∈o.
(1) bL(xy) = bL(x)bLx(y);
(2) bL(x) = bLx(x)−1;
(3) bL(x) = bL(y) if x, y are both expressions for some w ∈W.

Blmin : M o(W) →M o(W).

Example 2.1.8.
(1) Let o = 1. Then W ◦
1 = W.

Example 2.1.9.
(1) Endoscopic Coxeter subgroups need not be parabolic subgroups of W. Let
W be the Coxeter group of type B2, with generators s and t. Let W ′ be the reflection subgroup
generated by the reflections s and tst. Note that W ′ is of type A1 × A1 and clearly is not a
parabolic subgroup of W. We can then take o = W ′\W. By Example 2.1.8 (5), we have that
W ◦
e = W ′.
(2) Endoscopic Coxeter subgroups need not be finite rank Coxeter groups even when W has finite
rank. Let W be the universal Coxeter group on three generators s1, s2, s3. Consider the element
x = s1s2, which has infinite order. Define an infinite sequence of reflections rn = xns3x−n for
all n ∈Z≥0. Let W ′ be the reflection subgroup of W generated by {rn}n≥0. Here we have
that W ′ is isomorphic to the free product of countably infinitely many copies of Z/2Z. It can
then be easily verified that W ′ is not isomorphic to a Coxeter group with finitely many simple
reflections. Again, we can take o = W ′\W and Example 2.1.8 (5) shows that W ◦
e = W ′.
Definition 2.1.10. An expression w ∈Exp(W) of w ∈W ◦
L is said to be endo-reduced if ℓL(w) = ℓL(w).
Definition 2.1.11. An endosimple expansion datum for L is a function
ι : S◦
L →Exp(W)
such that ι(s) is an endo-reduced expression for s ∈W.
Given an endosimple expansion datum ι for L, then ι can be extended uniquely to all W ◦
L-expressions,
ι : Exp(W ◦
L) →Exp(W).
Moreover, ι satisfies that if x is a reduced expression for some x ∈W ◦
L, then ι(x) is an endo-reduced
expression in W.
2.2. Realizations. For both Bott–Samelson bimodules and the diagrammatic category, the starting
point will be the data of a realization of a Coxeter system. In order for the resulting categories to
be well-defined, one has to impose some rather technical combinatorial constraints. We will cover the
necessary combinatorics in this section.
2.2.1. Two-Colored Quantum Numbers. Let A = Z[x, y] be the integral polynomial ring in two vari-
ables. We define a collection of elements of A called the two-colored quantum numbers as follows. First
set [0]x = 0, [0]y = 0, [1]x = 1, [1]y = 1, [2]x = x, and [2]y = y. Define the remaining quantum
numbers recursively by the formulas
(2.2.1)
[n + 1]x = [2]x[n]y −[n −1]x
and
[n + 1]y = [2]y[n]x −[n −1]y.
One can use the same formulas to define [n]x and [n]y for n ≤0. It is easy to check that [−n]x = −[n]x
and [−n]y = −[n]y. For all d ∈Z, we define the d-twisted variables,
x(d) = [d + 1]x −[d −1]x
and
y(d) = [d + 1]y −[d −1]y.


===== PDF PAGE 11 =====

SOERGEL CALCULUS FOR MONODROMIC HECKE CATEGORIES

For all n ∈Z, we will write [n]x(d) (resp. [n]y(d)) for the element of A obtained by substituting x with
x(d) and y with y(d) in [n]x (resp. [n]y).
We now recall some standard facts about two-colored quantum numbers (cf., [Eli16,EW16,EW23]).
Lemma 2.2.2. Let n, m ∈Z.
(1) If n is odd, then [n]x = [n]y. As a result, if d is even, then x(d) = y(d).
(2) If either n is even or both n, m are odd, then

(3) If n divides m, then [n]x divides [m]x.
(4) We have that [2n]x = [n]x([n + 1]x −[n −1]x).
It will be useful to give a recursive formula for computing the d-twisted variables similar to the
two-colored quantum numbers. We write x′(0) = 2, y′(0) = 2, x′(1) = x, and y′(1) = y. We can then

Lemma 2.2.3. For all d ≥0, we have that x′(d) = x(d) and y′(d) = y(d).
Proof. We will just prove that x′(d) = x(d). The other statements follow from an entirely symmetric
argument. We will argue by induction on d. If d = 0, then x(0) = 1 −(−1) = 2. If d = 1, then
x(1) = [2]x = x. Assume that x′(k) = x(k) for k < d and d ≥2. If d is even, then
x′(d) = −x′(d −2) + y · x′(d −1)
= −x(d −2) + y · x(d −1)
= −[d −1]x −[d −3]x + y[d]x −y[d −2]x
= (y[d]x −[d −1]y) −(y[d −2]x −[d −3]y)
= [d + 1]y −[d −1]y
= [d + 1]x −[d −1]x.
The case of d odd is similar, and thus omitted.
□

2.2.6. Rank 2 Reflection Subgroups of Dihedral Groups. Let m ∈Z≥0 ∪{∞}. Let Wm denote the
dihedral group of order 2m.
If m ≥2, we regard Wm as a Coxeter group via the presentation
⟨s, t | s2 = t2 = (st)m⟩. More generally, we interpret W0 as the trivial group and W1 as the type A1
Coxeter group.
The following lemma is well-known and can be proved using basic group theory.
Lemma 2.2.7. Assume m ∈Z≥0 ∪{∞}. Let W ′ be a rank ≤2 reflection subgroup of Wm. If m < ∞,
then W ′ is isomorphic as a Coxeter group to Wv for some v dividing m or v = 0. If m = ∞, then W ′
is an infinite dihedral group.
If o is a Wm-set and L ∈o, the endoscopic subgroup W ◦
L is a reflection subgroup.

2.2.8. Abe Realizations. Let k be an integral domain. Let a, b ∈k. Consider the ring homomorphism
A →k[x, y]/(x −a, y −b) ∼= k defined by the canonical map Z →k. Given p ∈A, we will write p(a, b)
for the element of k obtained via this homomorphism. At times, when the choice of a, b are obvious,
we will simply refer to p as an element of k.
Definition 2.2.9. Let k be an integral domain. A pre-realization of a Coxeter group (W, S) over k
consists of a free, finite rank k-module h along with subsets
{α∨
s : s ∈S} ⊂h
and
{αs : s ∈S} ⊂h∗,
called the simple coroots and simple roots respectively, such that
(1) ⟨α∨
s , αs⟩= 2 for all s ∈S;
(2) the assignment
s(v) = v −⟨v, αs⟩α∨
s
for all s ∈S and v ∈h defines a representation of W on h;
(3) αs : h →k is surjective for all s ∈S.
Here we are writing ⟨−, −⟩: h × h∗→k for the canonical pairing. The third condition is called
Demazure surjectivity.
To every pre-realization h, there is a Cartan matrix (as,t)s,t∈S where as,t := ⟨α∨
s , αt⟩. We will
further call a pre-realization h an Abe realization if for all distinct s, t ∈S such that st has order

Hazi has shown that in the absence of parabolic subgroups of type H3, the diagrammatic Hecke
category is well-defined if and only if the underlying realization is an Abe realization [Haz24]. We
will later see that the diagrammatic monodromic Hecke category may not be well-defined, even if the
underlying realization is an Abe realization. To resolve this, we introduce a collection of modified
versions of the Abe realization condition.
Definition 2.2.10. Let h be a pre-realization over k of a Coxeter group (W, S). Let o be a right
W-set. We call h an endoscopic Abe realization if h is an Abe realization of (W ◦
L, S◦
L) for all L ∈o.
Definition 2.2.11. Let h be a pre-realization over k of a Coxeter group (W, S). Let o be a right
W-set. We call h a reflection-stable Abe realization if for all distinct s, t ∈S with ms,t < ∞and

Since we are primarily concerned with knowing when the realization h for W restricts to an Abe
realization for a reflection subgroup W ′, we do not particularly care what choice of simple roots and
coroots is needed. This in addition with Lemma 2.2.15 (2) empowers us to suppress the reflection
datum when discussing restricted pre-realizations to W ′. In particular, we will call any of the pre-
realizations arising via Lemma 2.2.15 (1) the restricted pre-realization even though it does depend on
a choice. This abuse of terminology will be fully rectified in Lemma 2.2.29.
The following two lemmas easily follow from the definitions.
Lemma 2.2.16. Let (W, S) be a Coxeter system and let h be a pre-realization for W. Let P be one
of the following properties of a (pre-)realization: monodromic Abe, reflection-stable Abe, or Abe. The
following are equivalent
(1) h is a P realization for W{s,t} for distinct s, t ∈S;
(2) h is a P realization for W;
(3) h is a P realization for WI for all I ⊆S;
(4) h is a P realization for xWI := xWIx−1 for all x ∈W and I ⊆S.
Lemma 2.2.17. Let (W, S) be a Coxeter system and let h be a pre-realization for W. Let (W ′, S′) be
a reflection subgroup of W and let x ∈W. Then h is an Abe realization for (W ′, S′) if and only if h
is an Abe realization for (xW ′x−1, xS′x−1).
Lemma 2.2.18. Let (W, S) be a Coxeter group and W ′ is a reflection subgroup of W with canonical
generators S′. Assume that W ′ is a finite Coxeter group of rank r. Then there exists a rank r parabolic
subgroup wWI of W such that W ′ ⊆wWI.
Proof. It is a classical result that W ′ is contained in some finite parabolic subgroup of W (see [Bou68,
Page 130]). As a result, without loss of generality, we may assume that W itself is finite. The result
then follows from [DPR13, Lemma 2.1].
□

Proposition 2.2.21. Let (W, S) be a Coxeter group and W ′ be a reflection subgroup of W with
canonical generators S′. Let h be a reflection-stable Abe realization. Then h is an Abe realization for
W ′.
Proof. By Lemma 2.2.15, h is a pre-realization for W ′. Let s′, t′ ∈S′ such that s′t′ has finite order
(greater than 1). Write W ′
{s′,t′} for the parabolic subgroup of W ′ generated by s′, t′. By Lemma 2.2.16,
it suffices to show that h is an Abe realization for W ′
{s′,t′}. Note that W ′
{s′,t′} is a rank-2 reflection
subgroup of W. By Lemma 2.2.18, we can find some w ∈W and s, t ∈S such that W ′
{s′,t′} ⊆wW{s,t}.
By another application of Lemma 2.2.16, we have that h is a reflection-stable Abe realization for
wW{s,t}. Then h is an Abe realization for W ′
{s′,t′} by Lemma 2.2.20.
□
Corollary 2.2.22. Let (W, S) be a Coxeter system and o be a W-set.
Any reflection-stable Abe
realization of W is also an endoscopic Abe realization.
Proof. The claim follows from Proposition 2.2.21 after the observation that every endoscopic Coxeter
group is a reflection subgroup (see Proposition 2.1.6).
□
Remark 2.2.23. Proposition 2.2.21 implies that h being reflection-stable is sometimes a necessary
condition for h to be endoscopic. Indeed, by Example 2.1.8 (5), every reflection subgroup can arise as
an endoscopic Coxeter group. As a result, we can pick o such that every reflection subgroup of W is
of the form W ◦
L for some L ∈o. On the other extreme, we can always consider the non-monodromic
case where o = 1. Here, being reflection-stable is substantially stronger than being endoscopic.

Proof. If W L

s,t. If W L
s,t is of rank 2, then
we are done by Lemma 2.2.20.
□

2.2.26. Balanced Realizations. Let (W, S) be a Coxeter group. We saw in the previous section that
the restriction of a realization h of W to a reflection subgroup W ′ ⊆W is not strictly well-defined. In
particular, it depends on the choice of a reflection datum. In practice, keeping track of this additional
data becomes quite tedious. As a result, we will usually restrict our attention to balanced realizations.
This ensures that the restriction to a reflection subgroup does not depend on the reflection datum.
However, it is not clear whether the restricted realization is also balanced. To compensate, we will
impose a further condition that ensures all restricted realizations are also balanced.
The constraint that our realizations are balanced is not strictly necessary. For the theory of Soergel
bimodules, we will not use it at all. For the diagrammatic theory, using balanced realizations will
greatly simplify our constructions.
Definition 2.2.27. Let (W, S) be a Coxeter group with pre-realization h. We say that h is balanced
if
[ms,t −1]x(as,t, at,s) = 1 = [ms,t −1]y(as,t, at,s)
for all s, t ∈S.
We further say that h is reflection-balanced if
[ms,t/d −1]x(as,t, at,s) = 1 = [ms,t/d −1]y(as,t, at,s)
for all non-trivial divisors d of ms,t.
Remark 2.2.28. If h is balanced, then [ms,t −k]x(as,t, at,s) = [k]x(as,t, at,s) for all k. Using Proposi-
tion 2.2.4, we can then see that a sufficient but not necessary constraint for h to be reflection-balanced
is if [d]x(as,t, at,s) ̸= 0 for all proper divisors d of ms,t.
Lemma 2.2.29 ([Pat25, Lemma 1.2]). Let (W, S) be a Coxeter group with a balanced pre-realization
h. Let r be a reflection in W. Assume that r = xsx−1 = yty−1 for some x, y ∈W, s, t ∈S such that
xs > x and yt > y. Then xαs = yαt.
It immediately follows from Lemma 2.2.29 that if h is balanced, that the restriction of h to any
reflection subgroup is independent of the choice of reflection datum.
Proposition 2.2.30. Let (W, S) be a Coxeter group with a reflection-balanced reflection-stable Abe
realization h. Let (W ′, S′) be a reflection subgroup of W. Then h is a balanced Abe realization for
(W ′, S′).
Proof. Let s′, t′ ∈S′ such that s′t′ has order v < ∞. By Lemma 2.2.18, there exists some s, t ∈S and
x ∈W such that ⟨s′, t′⟩⊂xWs,t. Note that h is a balanced realization for xWs,t. By conjugation, we
may assume that s′ = xsx−1 and t′ = x(ts)d−1tx−1 for some d ≥1 with m = dv. By Lemma 2.2.29,
αs′ = xαs and αt′ = x(t(d−1))αu where u = t if d is odd and u = s if d is even. From this description,
we have that as′,t′ = ⟨α∨
s′, αt′⟩= ⟨α∨
s , t(d −1)αu⟩and at′,s′ = ⟨α∨
t′, αs′⟩= ⟨t(d −1)α∨
u, αs⟩. We will
now prove that
[v −1]x(as′,t′, at′,s′) = 1 = [v −1]y(as′,t′, at′,s′)
using a case-by-case analysis on v and d. The second equality follows from the same equality as the
first.

If v is even, then [v −1]x ∈Z[xy]. Since [v −1]x is concentrated in even degrees, by Lemma 2.2.19,
we have equalities [v −1]x(as′,t′, at′,s′) = [v −1]x(d)(as,t, at,s) = 1.
If v is odd, then d must also be odd divisor. By Lemma 2.2.19, we have equalities (x(d))(as,t, at,s) =
as′,t′ and (y(d))(as,t, at,s) = at′,s′. Therefore, [v −1]x(as′,t′, at′,s′) = [v −1]x(d)(as,t, at,s) = 1.
□

3. Monodromic Soergel Bimodules
A foundational tool for working with the Elias–Williamson diagrammatic category is the Soergel
functor
(3.0.1)
DBS(h, W) →BSBim(h, W)
from the diagrammatic Hecke category to Bott–Samelson bimodules. For finite Coxeter groups, suf-
ficiently nice realizations over k, and certain small primes being invertible in k, this functor is an
equivalence of categories. Problems quickly arise when any of these three constraints are removed.
For our later applications, it will be crucial to have a well-behaved version of (3.0.1) which works for
arbitrary W and k. Fortunately, a solution to this problem already exists. In [Abe21], Abe had the in-
genious idea to modify the category of Bott–Samelson bimodules by keeping track of certain localization
data. The resulting category ABS(h, W) of Abe–Bott–Samelson bimodules gives a bimodule-theoretic
incarnation of the Elias–Williamson category. In particular, by [Abe21], there is an equivalence of
categories
(3.0.2)
DBS(h, W)
∼
→ABS(h, W).
The goal of this section is to construct a monodromic version of Abe–Bott–Samelson bimodules.
3.1. Abe–Soergel Theory. We will closely follow the approach of [Abe21], [Abe24], and [RV24].
The main difference between our presentation and that of loc. cit. will be a 2-categorical enrichment
which keeps track of monodromy parameters.
Fix a Coxeter system (W, S), a set of monodromy parameters o, and a reflection-balanced reflection-
stable Abe realization h of W.
We denote R for the symmetric algebra of h∗over k. We view R as a Z-graded k-algebra where h∗
is concentrated in degree 2. Consider the localized ring
Q := R

αw
: w ∈R(W)

The roots αw are well-defined by Lemma 2.2.29. The ring Q is naturally Z-graded. Moreover, the
W-action on h∗induces actions on R and on Q by graded algebra automorphisms.

Lemma 3.2.1. Let w ∈Exp(W). The module BL

of w by taking tensor products.

φ(a ⊗bw−1(r)) = a ⊗w(bw−1(r)) = a ⊗w(b)

φ(aw(f) ⊗b) = aw(f) ⊗w(b) = a ⊗w(fb) = φ(a ⊗fb).

As a result, there is an induced morphism of R-bimodules

It is easy to check that as left R-modules, both Rw ⊗Rs Rw−1 and R ⊗Rwsw−1 R are free R-modules of
rank 2. Moreover, since w·(−) : R →R takes s-(anti)invariants to wsw−1-(anti)invariants, it is easy to

Rw ⊗R Cs ⊗R Rw−1 ∼= Rw ⊗Rs Rw−1(1)
φ→R ⊗Rwsw−1 R(1) = Cwsw−1.
Clearly, this isomorphism takes 1 ⊗1 ⊗1 ⊗1 to 1 ⊗1. Since the degree 0 endomorphism algebra of
bimodule morphisms is rank 1, the isomorphism is uniquely determined.

3.4. Morphisms of Bimodules. We will define a collection of morphisms which we will later show

3.4.1. 1-Color Morphisms. Let s ∈S such that Ls = L.

4. Algebraic Light Leaves
The goal of this section is to extend the construction of light leaves for Bott–Samelson bimodules
(cf., [Lib08,Abe21]) to monodromic Bott–Samelson bimodules. We closely follow the construction and
proofs given in [Abe21]. We also provide three main applications of light leaves:
(1) a version of Soergel’s Hom formula to compute the graded rank of homomorphisms in the
category of Bott–Samelson bimodules;
(2) a basis of the Hom spaces between Bott–Samelson bimodules;
(3) a cellular structure on Bott–Samelson bimodules.
In order to use the morphism afforded by Proposition 3.4.3, we will assume throughout this section
that h is a reflection-balanced monodromic Abe realization.
4.1. Preliminaries on Monodromic Subexpressions.
4.1.1. Subexpressions and Order. Let w = (s1, . . . , sk) be an expression in W. Recall the set,
K(w, L) = {1 ≤i ≤k | Ls1 . . . si = Ls1 . . . si−1}.
We will denote by Sub(w) the set of subexpressions of w. For a given x ∈W, we can consider the
subset Sub(w, x) consisting of subexpressions e of w such that we = x.
A subexpression e = (e1, . . . , ek) of w is said to be (L-)monodromic if ei = 1 whenever i /∈K(w, L).
We will denote by SubL(w) the subset of Sub(w) consisting of L-monodromic subexpressions. Similarly,
for x ∈W, we will write SubL(w, x) = SubL(w) ∩Sub(w, x).
Let w = (s1, . . . , sk) ∈Exp(W) and e ∈Sub(w). For each i = 1, . . . , k, we write w≤i (resp. e≤i) for
the first i terms of w (resp. e). Then e≤i is a subexpression of w≤i. Let wi be the element expressing
w≤i. We can define the i-th label, labi(w, e) ∈{U, D} by
labi(w, e) :=
(
U
if wi−1si > wi−1,
D
if wi−1si < wi−1.
The i-th decoration of e, denoted deci(w, e) ∈{U0, U1, D0, D1}, is defined by
deci(w, e) := labi(w, e)ei.
The defect of e, denoted d(w, e), is the integer defined by
d(w, e) := #{i | deci(w, e) = U0} −#{i | deci(w, e) = D0}.
For x ∈W, we can define a relation <, called the label-lexicographic order, on Sub(w, x) by f < e
if and only if there exists some 1 ≤i = i(e, f) ≤k such that
(1) labj(w, e) = labj(w, f) for all j < i,
(2) labi(w, e) = D and labi(w, f) = U.


===== PDF PAGE 29 =====

SOERGEL CALCULUS FOR MONODROMIC HECKE CATEGORIES
29
By [Abe21, Lemma 3.9], the relation < gives a total order on Sub(w, x). Moreover, if e and f are
L-monodromic subexpressions, then it follows from the definitions that i(e, f) ∈K(w, L). As a result,
the relation < restricts to a total order on SubL(w, x).
We define another relation on subexpressions, although this time without a fixed evaluation. Let
e, f be two subexpressions of w ∈Exp(W). Let x0, x1, . . . , xm and y0, y1, . . . , ym denote the Bruhat
strolls of e and f, respectively. We say that e ≺f if xi ≤yi for all 0 ≤i ≤m. This defines a partial
order ≺on Sub(w) called the path dominance order. The path dominance order restricts to a partial
order on monodromic subexpressions.

is far from canonical. We define an LL-datum as a triple (rex, {rexs}s∈S, {Γx,y}x,y) consisting of the
following:
(1) a map rex : W →Exp(W) such that for each x ∈W, rex(x) is a reduced expression for x;

(2) for each s ∈S, a map rexs : Rs →Exp(W) where Rs = {w ∈W | ℓ(ws) < ℓ(w)} and rexs(x)

(3) for any two reduced expressions x and y for x, a path Γx,y from x to y in the rex graph of x.

4.7. Localization and Cellular Structure. In this section, we will discuss some techniques for
studying Bott–Samelson bimodules via localization. We will then use localization to further study
light leaves and double leaves. Our main result will be a generalization of Theorem 4.6.2 for the “ideal
of lower terms” which gives a cellular structure on the category of Bott–Samelson bimodules.
4.7.1. The Localization Functor. For each L, L′ ∈o, we define a 1-category
L\CQ/L′(h, W, o) whose
objects consist of tuples (P w)w∈W such that
(1) P w is a graded Q-bimodule;
(2) m · q = w(q) · m for any m ∈P w and q ∈Q;
(3) P w = 0 if w /∈LWL′ and is only nonzero for finitely many w ∈LWL′.
A morphism in L\CQ/L′(h, W, o) from (P w
1 )w∈W to (P w
2 )w∈W consists of a tuple (φw)w∈W where each
φw : P w
1 →P w
2 is a Q-bimodule homomorphism.
There are bifunctors
(−) ⊗Q (−) : L\CQ/L′(h, W, o) × L′\CQ/L′′(h, W, o) →L\CQ/L′′(h, W, o)
induced from the tensor product of graded Q-bimodules. More precisely, (P1⊗QP2)w = L
xy=w P x
1 ⊗Q
P y
2 . These bifunctors are suitably associative and there is a unit object Qe given by (Qe)e = Q and


===== PDF PAGE 37 =====

SOERGEL CALCULUS FOR MONODROMIC HECKE CATEGORIES
37
(Qe)w = 0 if w ̸= e. The tensor product allows us to assemble a 2-category \CQ/(h, W, o) with object
set o and whose morphism categories are given by
L\CQ/L′(h, W, o). We sometimes omit the tuple
(h, W, o) from the notation for L\CQ/L′(h, W, o) and \CQ/(h, W, o).
The assignment (M, (M w
Q)w∈W , ξM) 7→(M w
Q)w∈W defines a 2-functor
(−)Q : \C/(h, W, o) →\CQ/(h, W, o)
called the localization functor. Since M →M ⊗R Q is injective (M is free over R), the localization
functor is faithful on morphism categories.
4.7.2. Localizing Bott–Samelson Bimodules. For w ∈W, we define Qw ∈L\CQ/Lw(h, W, o) by
• (Qw)w = Q as a left Q-module and the right action of q ∈Q is given by m · q = w(q) · m;
• (Qw)x = 0 if x ̸= w.
Any object in

unique Q-bimodule morphism taking 1 ⊗1 to 1.

5. Monodromic Hecke Category
Let (W, S) be a Coxeter system and o a set of monodromy parameters for W. We will fix a reflection-
balanced reflection-stable Abe realization h of W. By Corollary 2.2.22 and Proposition 2.2.30, we have
that h is a balanced Abe realization of W ◦
L for all L ∈o as well.

We will define numerous 2-categories in this section. To keep notation consistent, unless otherwise
stated, ◦will refer to the vertical composition and ⋆will refer to the horizontal composition in a
2-category.
5.1. Face Colorings of Elias–Williamson Graphs.
Definition 5.1.1. An Elias–Williamson graph is a planar graph Γ embedded on the planar strip
R × [0, 1] with the following data:
(1) the edges of Γ are colored by elements of S;
(2) the faces of Γ are decorated by boxes labelled by homogenous f ∈R;
(3) the vertices of Γ are of 3 types:
(a) univalent vertices,
(b) trivalent vertices, where all three adjoining edges have the same color,
(c) 2ms,t-valent vertices, where the adjoining edges alternate in color between two elements
s ̸= t ∈S.
Given an Elias–Williamson graph, we define its degree as the sum of the degrees of each vertex, and
the degrees of each polynomial. The degrees on vertices and boxes are given as follows:
(5.1.1)
f
1
−1
0
deg(f)
Degree:
Definition 5.1.2. A monodromic Elias–Williamson graph is an Elias–Williamson graph with an ad-
ditional coloring of the faces by elements of o such that if two faces colored L ∈o and L′ ∈o are
adjacent along an edge colored by s ∈S, then L′ = Ls.
We can define the degree of a monodromic Elias–Williamson graph as the degree of its underlying
Elias–Williamson graph.
Example 5.1.3. Let W = S3 × S2 with simple reflections S = {s, t, u} where (st)3 = 1. Define a
right W-set o = {1, 2, 3} by
1 · s = 1,
2 · s = 3,
1 · t = 2,
3 · t = 3,
1 · u = 1,
2 · u = 2,
3 · u = 3.
An example of a monodromic Elias–Williamson graph for (W, o) is given below.
αs
Convention 5.1.4. The face coloring of a monodromic Elias–Williamson graph is uniquely determined
by the coloring of any face in the underlying Elias–Williamson graph. As a result, we will not always
color every face of a monodromic Elias–Williamson graph. If the left most color is clear from context,
we often will only draw the underlying Elias–Williamson graph. At times, we will color only key parts
of a monodromic Elias–Williamson graph to highlight important behavior.
We denote the set of isotopy classes of Elias–Williamson graphs by SGr and the set of isotopy classes
of monodromic Elias–Williamson graphs by µSGr. If L, L′ ∈o, we will write LµSGrL′ for the subset
of µSGr consisting of graphs whose most left face is colored by L and whose right most face is colored
by L′.
Let x, y ∈Exp(W). We can consider the subset SGr(x, y) of SGr consisting of Elias–Williamson
graphs with bottom boundary x and top boundary y.
We similarly define a subset µSGr(x, y) of
µSGr consisting of monodromic Elias–Williamson graphs whose underlying Elias–Williamson graph is
in SGr(x, y). Likewise, we denote LµSGrL′(x, y) := µSGr(x, y) ∩LµSGrL′.

5.2.4. The Universal Calculus. In the non-monodromic setting, one usually goes straight from the
1-color diagrammatics to 2-colors diagrammatics when defining Hecke categories.
However, in the
monodromic setting, it is useful to introduce an intermediary where we allow for arbitrary 1-morphisms,

but only consider 2-morphisms corresponding to rank 1 parabolic subgroups. This has the advantage of
allowing for monodromy parameters to play a more central role– giving way to glimpses of endoscopy.

the universal category.

on the set S and its action on o, but does not include any Coxeter theoretic information. In other
words, the universal one-color category does not include information on rank 2 and rank 3 parabolic
subgroups of W.
The main advantage of introducing the universal category is that it contains many interesting

5.3. The Monodromic Dihedral Cathedral. The goal of this section is to define the diagrammatic
monodromic Hecke category for Coxeter groups of type I2(m) for m < ∞. In terms of generators,
this amounts to adding 2m-valent vertices to Elias–Williamson graphs. The relations amongst these
generators will combine the one-color relations along with two new relations: two-color associativity
and the Elias–Jones–Wenzl relation. Two-color associativity is a straightforward generalization of the
corresponding non-monodromic concept. The Elias–Jones–Wenzl relation is much more subtle. In the
non-monodromic setting, the Elias–Jones–Wenzl relation is defined using Jones–Wenzl projectors for
the two-colored Temperley–Lieb algebra. Unfortunately, we do not have a good notion of a monodromic
Temperley–Lieb algebra. Instead, we will consider an endoscopic Temperley–Lieb algebra and then
apply endosimple expansion. This has the benefit of allowing us to use known results on Temperley–
Lieb algebras.
Before continuing to the construction of the Jones–Wenzl projectors. We will set up some notation
which will be used throughout this section. Let Wm denote the dihedral group with simple reflections
s, t where m = ms,t < ∞. Let o be a Wm-set. We will write W L
s,t for the endoscopic subgroup of Wm
corresponding to L ∈o. Note that W L
s,t has rank 0, 1, or 2. When W L
s,t is rank 2, we will write s′, t′ for
the endosimple reflections in W L
s,t. Moreover, we will write v = vL
s,t for the order of s′t′ which divides
m. We then have that W L
s,t is isomorphic as a Coxeter group to Wv.
5.3.1. Temperley–Lieb Category. Let A be a Z[x, y]-algebra. We will review the construction of the two-
colored Temperley–Lieb 2-category 2T LA and discuss some properties. There is no new mathematical
content here and a more detailed treatise can be found elsewhere (cf., [EW16,EW23,Haz24]).
Definition 5.3.2. The two-colored Temperley–Lieb 2-category 2T LA is the 2-category defined as
follows. There are two objects of 2T LA which are denoted by s and t. The 1-morphisms of 2T LA
consist of finite alternating sequences (. . . , s, t, s, t, . . .). For an integer n ≥0, we write sn for the
sequence of length n + 1 which begins with s, and tn for the sequence of length n + 1 which begins
with t. We visualize the 1-morphisms as a sequence of n dots on a line whose regions are alternately
colored by s and t. E.g.,
s5 =
There are no 2-morphisms between sn and tn′ in either direction. Let u ∈{s, t}. The A-module
of 2-morphisms 2- Hom2T L(un, un′) is given by the free A-module whose basis CM(un, un′) consists
of two colored (n, n′)-crossingless matchings. Vertical composition (◦) of 2-morphisms is defined by
concatenation of crossingless matchings along with the following “bubble evaluation” rule,

Definition 5.3.3. Let u ∈{s, t}. An element JWA(un) ∈TLA(un) is called a two-colored Jones–

Lemma 5.3.9. Let u′ ∈{s′, t′} and φ : u′n →u′n′ be a 2-morphism in 2T LA.
Then ΣL(φ) is
(d(n + 1), d(n′ + 1))-adjustable.
Proof. Without loss of generality, we may take u′ = s′. Let N (resp. N ′) be the integer such that
ι(s′n + 1) = sN (resp. ι(s′n′ + 1) = sN ′). If φ = 0, there is nothing to prove.
Note that d = 1
2(ℓ(s′) + ℓ(t′)). If n is odd, then φ being nonzero implies that n′ is also odd. In
this case, N = d(n + 1) and N ′ = d(n′ + 1) since s′ and t′ appear the same number of times in sn + 1
(resp. sn′ + 1). As a result, ΣL(φ) is trivially (d(n + 1), d(n′ + 1))-truncatable (and extendable).
Suppose n is even. Again, φ ̸= 0 implies that n′ is also even. In this case, N = dn + ℓ(s′) and
N ′ = dn′ + ℓ(s′). Write r = 1
2(ℓ(t′) −ℓ(s′)). First, assume that ℓ(s′) ≤ℓ(t′), so that r ≥0. We then
have that N + r = d(n + 1) and N ′ + r = d(n′ + 1). As a result, ΣL(φ) ⋆idBL
tr : BL
sd(n+1) →BL
sd(n′+1),
and thus φ is (d(n + 1), d(n′ + 1))-extendable. Now assume that ℓ(s′) > ℓ(t′), so that r < 0. From the
definition of endosimple expansion given in Proposition 5.2.7, we can see that ΣL(φ) is of the form
f ′ ⋆idBL
tc where c = 1
2(ℓ(s′) −1) (since these c-terms are minimal in their corresponding block). Then
c ≥r, so we have that ΣL(φ) is (d(n + 1), d(n′ + 1))-truncatable.
□

5.4. Presentation of the Monodromic Hecke Category. We will now return to the general case
where (W, S) is an arbitrary Coxeter system and o is a W-set.
We are almost ready to give the definition of the monodromic Hecke category in full generality.
Before doing so, we first need to impose a technical constraint on our Coxeter systems to ensure that
the three-color relations are computable. The constraint is essentially identical to the usual constraint
that H3 does not appear as a parabolic subgroup for the non-monodromic Hecke category.
Assumption 5.4.1. For all type H3 standard parabolic subgroups WI of W and all L ∈o, we have
that WI ∩W ◦
L is a proper subgroup of WI.
5.4.2. Definition.
Definition 5.4.3. Let (W, S) be a Coxeter system and o be a W-set such that Assumption 5.4.1 is
satisfied. Let h be a reflection-balanced reflection-stable Abe realization for W. The diagrammatic
monodromic Hecke category, denoted \DBS
/ (h, W, o), is the k-linear strict 2-category defined as follows.
The object set is o. The 1-morphisms are tensor generated by 1-morphisms BL

(One-Color Relations) The relations of the one-color category (5.2.2).
(Two-color Associativity) The two-color associativity and Elias–Jones–Wenzl relations of (5.3.14).
(Zamolodchikov Equations) There is a 3-color relation for each finitary rank-3 subset I = {s, t, u}
of S. The specific form depends on the type of the parabolic subgroup WI along with its action on

Remark 5.4.4.
(1) Assumption 5.4.1 is needed since the non-monodromic Hecke category still
lacks a computation of the lower order terms needed for the corresponding three-color rela-
tion to hold.
Further discussion on this topic can be found in [EW23, §3.6].
Finding the
H3 Zamolodchikov equation in the non-monodromic Hecke category would also complete the
description in the monodromic Hecke category.
(2) It is somewhat surprising that lower order terms appear in the Zamolodchikov equations for
type B3 with an endoscopy subgroup of type A3. We can somewhat motivate this by the
monodromic-endoscopic equivalence. After endoscopy, the monodromic three-color relations
becomes (≤3)-color relations on the endoscopic side. The lower order terms can only appear
when the endoscopic subgroup also has rank 3. This happens quite rarely.
(a) In type A1 × I2(m), all endoscopic subgroups are of the following types: A1 × I2(v) and
I2(v) where 0 ≤v ≤m divides (by definition, we take I2(0) = 1 and I2(1) = A1).
(b) In type A3, all nontrivial endoscopic subgroups are of the following types: A3, A2, A1×A1,
and A1.
(c) In type B3, all nontrivial endoscopic subgroups are of the following types: B3, A3, A1 ×
A1 × A1, A1 × A2, A1 × A1, A2, B2, and A1.
(d) In type H3, all nontrivial endoscopic subgroups are of the following types: H3, A1 × A1 ×
A1, I2(5), A2, A1 × A1, and A1.


===== PDF PAGE 56 =====

56
COLTON SANDVIK
One way to interpret the lack of lower terms is that in almost every case the canonical orien-
tation of the rex graph on the monodromic side aligns with the canonical orientation of the
rex graph on the endoscopic side. There is exactly two places where this fails: (i) in type H3
when no canonical orientation exists, and (ii) in type B3 when the induced orientation on the
rex graph of A3 is not the canonical one.
Remark 5.4.5. If o = 1, then \DBS
/ (h, W, 1) is the non-monodromic diagrammatic Hecke category of
[EW16] viewed as a 2-category with one object.
Remark 5.4.6. It should be possible to define \DBS
/ (h, W, o) even when h is not reflection-balanced.
In particular, since h is reflection-stable, by [Haz24, Theorem B] one can still make sense of the Jones–
Wenzl projectors necessary to define the Jones–Wenzl morphisms. When h is not reflection-balanced,
these Jones–Wenzl morphisms fail to be rotation invariant, but instead are rotation invariant up to an
invertible scalar. To correct this, one must introduce two different 2m-valent vertices, and modify the
relations accordingly. Such a correction should be very close to the modifications done in [EW16, §5.6].

Claim 5.4.10. Υ is a well-defined 2-functor.
Proof. We have already shown that Υ is invariant under the 1-color relations in Proposition 5.2.3 and
under the 2-color relations in Claim 5.3.20. As a result, it only remains to check that Υ is invariant
under the 3-color relations.
We compute these case-by-case for types A3, B3, and H3. There are 30 possible monodromy labeling
in type A3, 98 possible monodromy labeling in type B3, and 164 possible monodromy labeling in type


===== PDF PAGE 57 =====

SOERGEL CALCULUS FOR MONODROMIC HECKE CATEGORIES
57
H3. Most of these cases, one can check by constraining Hom spaces or from existing literature that Υ
is invariant under the 3-color relations. The one exception is when B3 has an endoscopic subgroup of
type A3. This will be checked by hand using algebraic endoscopy.
In type A1 × I2(m), the possible endoscopic subgroups are of the form A1 × I2(v) and I2(v) where
0 ≤v ≤m divides m. When the coloring of the 3-color relation arises from an endoscopic subgroup
of rank ≤2, the invariance under Υ follows from constraints on Hom spaces in low rank. When the
endoscopic subgroup is rank 3, then the 3-color relation for A1 × I2(m) maps under Υ to the 3-color
relation for A1 × I2(v).
The complete proof is covered in Appendix A. Therein, we also show how the possible monodromy
parameters can be computed, some important examples, and how Υ-invariance can be computed.
□

5.5.7. Monodromic Abe Theorem. As a consequence of the double leaves basis for the category of Bott–
Samelson bimodules and the diagrammatic category, we deduce that Υ is an equivalence of categories.
This result generalizes [Abe21, Theorem 5.6] and [Abe24, Theorem 3.15].
Theorem 5.5.8. The 2-functor
Υ : \DBS
/ (h, W, o) →\ABS
/ (h, W, o)
is an equivalence of 2-categories.
Proof. It is clear that Υ is essentially surjective. Moreover, Υ takes double leaves to double leaves.
The result then follows from Theorem 4.6.2 and Theorem 5.5.4.
□

5.7.1. Object-Adapted Cellular Structure. In order to apply for formalism of Appendix B, we first must

Let I ⊆W and L, L′ ∈o. We set
LIL′ := {w ∈I | Lw = L′}.
The Bruhat order on W restricts to a partial order on LWL′. This induces an order topology on LWL′
from W. In particular, a subset K ⊂LWL′ is closed (resp. open) in LWL′ if and only if there exists a
closed (resp. open) subset I ⊂W such that K = LIL′.
Given w ∈W, we write {≤w} and {< w} for the closed subsets of W consisting of all elements
x ∈W such that x ≤w and x < w respectively.

Proof. We argue by induction on ℓ(w). The claim is obvious when w is reduced. Write w = xs for some

the claim holds for x. We then must show that if y is a reduced expression for some y ∈LWLws and
y ≤⋆x, then BL

{≤⋆w}/Lw.
If ℓ(y) < ℓ(x), then ℓ(ys) < ℓ(w), so by induction BL

5.7.11. Categorification. One application of the mixed derived category formalism is that it allows one
to prove that the monodromic Hecke category categorifies the monodromic Hecke algebra without any
constraint on k. Without the mixed derived category, standard techniques (cf., [EL16,EW16]) can be
used to show this categorification result, but only when k is a complete local ring or a field.

6. Diagrammatic Double Leaves Span
In order to complete the proof of Theorem 5.5.4, we must show that double leaves span. The proof
closely follows [EW16, §7]. Since the proof is quite lengthy and involved, we will just outline the key
statements and ideas when the arguments are virtually identical to [EW16].
For the remainder of the section, we will work with embedded graphs rather than isotopy classes
thereof. We abuse terminology and use the term monodromic Elias–Williamson graphs to refer to a
graph embedded without horizontal tangent lines so that no two vertices share the same y-coordinate.
It is convenient to assume that all monodromic Elias–Williamson graphs are embedded in the plane
R × [0, 1] such that R × {0} is the bottom of the graph and that R × {1} is the top of the graph.
6.1. Negative-Positive Decompositions. Let f be a monodromic Elias–Williamson graph.
We
define the height of f, denoted ht(f) as the number of generators needed to produce f (ignoring
polynomials). We also define the width of f at a y-coordinate a ∈[0, 1] (with no vertices on the line
y = a), denoted widtha(f), as the number of strands in f the line y = a passes through. The maxwidth
of f, denoted maxwidth(f), is the maximal width attained by f.
Given a generator, we assign an integer called the signed height as follows.
(6.1.1)
+2
+1
+1
0
−1
−1
−2
More generally, the negative height of a graph is the number of negative generators used with multi-
plicities given by (6.1.1). Similarly, the positive height is the number of positive generators used with
multiplicities given by (6.1.1). Note that the negative height and positive height of a map only de-
pends on the underlying Elias–Williamson graph and not on the choice of face labelling by monodromy
parameters. Moreover, light leaves have positive height 0.
We call a graph f : BL
w →BL
y (non-)negative (resp. (non-)positive) if f consists of solely (non-
)negative (resp. (non-)positive) generators. We say that f is negative-positive if it factors as f =
f2 ◦f1 with f2 non-negative and f1 non-positive. Furthermore, we call f strictly negative-positive if
widtha(f) < ℓ(w) for some a ∈[0, 1].
Given a morphism f in L\DBS
/L′(h, W, o), we say that f admits a negative-positive decomposition if it
can be expressed as a k-linear combination of negative-positive graphs. Similarly, f admits a strictly


===== PDF PAGE 65 =====

SOERGEL CALCULUS FOR MONODROMIC HECKE CATEGORIES
65
negative-positive decomposition if f can be expressed as a linear combination of strictly negative-
positive graphs.
The following lemma is a compilation of results from [EW16, §7.1] which are generalized to the
monodromic setting.
Lemma 6.1.1.
(1) Let s, t ∈S with m = ms,t < ∞. The monodromic Jones–Wenzl morphism
admits a decomposition JWL
s,t = idBL
sm +f where f has a strictly negative-positive whose terms

(2) Let f : BL

(3) If ϕ = fgh for f non-negative, h non-positive, and g having a (strictly) negative-positive
decomposition, then ϕ has a (strictly) negative-positive decomposition.
(4) Let w and w′ be rexes for w ∈W. Let β, β′ : BL

strictly negative-positive decomposition.
(5) Let x ∈Exp(W). Then idx has a negative-positive decomposition which factors through some
reduced expression w for some w such that there exists a subsequence e of x with xe = w.
Proof. (1):

argument then precedes as in [EW16, Claim 7.2].
(3):
This is a restatement of [EW16, Claim 7.3]. It is obvious from definitions.
(4):

(5):

When
x = (s1, . . . , sk) with si = si+1. We must consider both i ∈K(x, L) and i /∈K(x, L). In the former

(XM+1).
If M = 0, then a map has maxwidth 0 only when the underlying Elias–Williamson graph consists
only of polynomial boxes. As a result, (L0) and (X0) trivially hold.
We will now assume that M > 0.
Assume that ℓ(w) = M.

6.5.10. Attaching a 2m-valent Vertex. The last major component we need to complete the proof of

Lemma 6.5.11. Assume (LM−1). Let f : BL
x →BL
w be in XL

The non-monodromic variant of Lemma 6.5.11 is [EW16, Claim 7.13]. Our proof is quite differ-
ent from [EW16]. In fact, there is a subtle error in the proof provided by Elias–Williamson. The
general strategy is to reduce to the case of W being a dihedral group. From here, Elias–Williamson
appeal to the calculations of morphisms spaces done in [Eli16] using Soergel’s categorification theorem.
Unfortunately, [Eli16] crucially imposes additional constraints on realizations, namely that of “lesser
invertibility” and “local non-degeneracy”. These constraints are much stronger than the rather relaxed
constraints on realizations imposed in [EW16] or in this work. Our proof constitutes a corrected proof
of [EW16, Claim 7.13] by taking o = 1.

7. Geometric Monodromic Hecke Category
The celebrated Riche–Williamson theorem [RW18, Theorem 10.5] gives a monoidal equivalence of
categories
DBS(h, W)
∼
→ParBS(B\G/B, k)
from the graded Elias–Williamson diagrammatic category to the category of Bott–Samelson parity
sheaves on B\G/B where G is a complex reductive group and k is an integral domain.4
In this
section, we will establish a monodromic version of the Riche–Williamson theorem using the monodromic
diagrammatic category.
Due to issues with choosing representatives of minimal IC sheaves in the (geometric) monodromic
Hecke category, we will need to impose some constraints on our coefficient ring (see §7.2). Fix a finite
extension O of Zℓ. Denote by K its field of fractions and by F its residue field. The triple (K, O, F)
is called an ℓ-modular triple. For this section, we will require k ∈{K, O, F}. Note that k is either a
complete local ring or a field.
7.1. Preliminaries.
7.1.1. Flag Varieties. Let G be a connected complex reductive group. Fix a Borel B with Levi decom-
position B = T ⋉U. We denote by X (resp. Y) the character (resp. cocharacter) lattice of T. For any
integral domain k, one can consider the Kac–Moody realization h∗
k = k ⊗Z X and hk = k ⊗Z Y. Let
(W, S) denote the Weyl group of G with respect to T. For each s ∈S, there is a simple root αs ∈X
and a simple coroot α∨
s ∈Y. We often view the simple roots (resp. coroots) as elements of h∗
k (resp.
hk).
Define a ring Z′ as follows. If all the maps αs : Y →Z and α∨
s : X →Z are surjective, we set Z′ = Z,
and otherwise, we set Z′ = Z[ 1
2]. For the entirety of this section, we assume that there exists a ring
homomorphism Z′ →k. The triple (hk, {α∨
s }s∈S, {αs}s∈S) is a reflection-balanced reflection-stable
Abe realization (Proposition 2.2.25 and Remark 2.2.31). Demazure surjectivity for hk is guaranteed
from the ring homomorphism Z′ →k. Since multiple coefficient rings will appear throughout this
section, we will write Rk = Sym(h∗
k) instead of R to emphasize the dependence on k.
Let f
Fℓ:= U\G denote the enhanced flag variety of G. Recall that the Bruhat decomposition
f
Fℓ=
G
w∈W
f
Fℓw
gives a stratification of f
Fℓwhere each f

We write jw : f

Fℓfor the inclusion map.
4The Riche–Williamson theorem is in fact more general and holds when G is a Kac–Moody group.

7.1.2. Hecke Categories. Let Ch(T, k) denote the set of rank 1 multiplicative local systems on T. A
local system L ∈Ch(T, k) is said to be torsion if there exists some n ∈Z≥0 which is invertible in k such
that L⊗n ∼= kT . We denote the subset of Ch(T, k) consisting of torsion local systems by Chµ(T, k).
We also fix a W-orbit o in Chµ(T, k).
Let L, L′ ∈o. We can view L′ as a multiplicative local system on B via pullback along B →T. We
can then define the derived category
L\D/L′(G, k) := Dc(T\L f
Fℓ/L′B, k)
of (T × B, L ⊠(L′)−1)-equivariant sheaves on f
Fℓ.
For a precise construction of this category see
[San25]. As we vary L and L′, we can assemble L\D/L′(G, k) into a 2-category, denoted \D/(G, k, o),
whose object set is o, and whose morphism categories are given by
L\D/L′(G, k) for L, L′ ∈o. The
horizontal composition in \D/(G, k, o) is by the convolution product ⋆(see [San25, §3.3]).
For each w ∈W and lift ˙w ∈NG(T) of w, by [San25, Proposition 3.2.2], there is a perverse t-exact
equivalence of categories
L\D/Lw(w, k) := Dc(T\L f
Fℓw/LwB, k) ∼= Dc(T\ pt, k)
given by taking the stalk at ˙w.
We can then define an object KL
˙w in
L\D/Lw(w, k) as the sheaf
corresponding to the constant sheaf in Dc(T\ pt, k) under the above equivalence.5 For two different
lifts ˙w, ˙w′ of w, there is a non-unique isomorphism KL
˙w
∼
→KL
˙w′. There are standard, costandard, and
simple perverse sheaves defined by
∆L
˙w := jw!KL
˙w[ℓ(w)],
∇L
˙w := jw∗KL
˙w[ℓ(w)],
ICL
˙w = Im(∆L
˙w →∇L
˙w),
respectively. These are all perverse sheaves in L\D/Lw(G, k). We will frequently replace the ˙w with w
in the notation for these sheaves to refer to any object in their isomorphism class rather than a fixed
representative.
Remark 7.1.3.
(1) There is a canonical representative of ICL
e given by the canonical lift of e in
NG(T).
(2) Let s ∈S such that Ls = L. Consider the rank 1 Levi subgroup Ls of G corresponding to
s. There is a unique extension of L to Ls, denoted Ls ([San25, Lemma 3.2.4]). As a result,
we have a canonical representative for ICL

Let L, L′ ∈o. There is a forgetful functor

7.2. Minimal IC Sheaves. For each block β ∈LW L′, there is a minimal element wβ under the
partial order ≤L. We can then consider the perverse sheaf ICL
wβ ∈
L\D/L′(G, k). A priori, these
sheaves are only defined up to (non-unique) isomorphism, and there is no canonical representative of
the isomorphism class. Our first step will be to choose representatives in a coherent manner such that
the resulting class is compatible with convolution. The work in picking these representatives is covered
by [San25, §5.8].
5The notation for objects is opposite to that of [San25] to match the presentation given for the diagrammatic and
algebraic Hecke categories. In particular, in loc. cit., KL
˙w refers to an object with right equivariant monodromy L whereas
here it refers to an object with left equivariant monodromy L.

Let Ξo(W) denote the groupoid of blocks. In more detail, Ξo(W) is a category whose object set is o
and whose morphism set from L to L′ is given by LW L′. In the following proposition, we view Ξo(W)
as a 2-category by setting all the 2-morphisms to be identities.
Proposition 7.2.1 ([San25, Corollary 5.8.11]). There exists a 2-functor
ICmin : Ξo(W) →\D/(G, k, o)
taking each β ∈LW L′ to a sheaf in the isomorphism class ICL
wβ.
We will fix a 2-functor ICmin : Ξo(W) →

remainder of the paper.
It is useful to unpack what this 2-functor entails.
First, for each block
β ∈LW L′, there is an object

bβ,γ : θL

→θL

These isomorphisms are associative in the sense that if δ ∈L′′W L′′′, there is an equality
bβγ,δ(bβ,γ × id) = bβ,γδ(id ×bγ,δ).
Remark 7.2.2. Proposition 7.2.1 is where we crucially use the assumption that k ∈{K, O, F}. The
reason for this assumption is that the construction of ICmin requires changing the sheaf-theoretic
setting to étale sheaves to make use of a Whittaker model.
The author conjectures that such a
2-functor exists under the same assumptions on k used throughout the paper (namely, noetherian
domain of finite global dimension). If such a 2-functor can be constructed, the rest of our results,
namely Theorem 7.8.1, can be easily derived using similar arguments for these more general coefficient
rings.

7.4. Maximal IC Sheaves. For this section, we require that k = K so that k is a field of characteristic
0. There are variations of the results of this section that hold whenever k = F provided certain small
primes are invertible. However, these variations require new proofs that are not provided in [San25],
and will not be need for the present work. Note that when k = K, the decomposition theorem implies
that L\Par/L′(G, K) is generated under direct sums and shifts by the simple perverse sheaves ICL
w for
w ∈LWL′.
Let β ∈LW L′ be a block. Since W is finite, the block β admits a maximal element wβ under the
partial order ≤β. We can then consider the perverse sheaf ICL
wβ ∈L\D/L′(G, K). As with the minimal
IC sheaves, these sheaves are only defined up to (non-unique) isomorphism. The goal of the section is
two-fold. First, we will use our choices of minimal IC sheaves to fix representatives of the maximal IC
sheaves. Second, we will construct a canonical “coalgebra” structure on the maximal IC sheaves.
7.4.1. Properties. The following two propositions are mild generalizations of [San25, Proposition 4.4.3]
and [San25, Proposition 4.4.5]. Related statements can be found in [LY20, Proposition 6.2] and [LY20,
Proposition 6.4]. We omit the proofs as they identical to the referenced propositions.

7.6. Constructing the Riche–Williamson Functor.
Lemma 7.6.1. Let k, k′ be noetherian domains of finite global dimension, and let φ : k →k′ be
a ring homomorphism. Let L ∈Chµ(T, k). For all w ∈W, one has that Lw = L if and only if
k′(L)w = k′(L).


===== PDF PAGE 82 =====

82
COLTON SANDVIK
Proof. If Lw = L, then clearly k′(L)w = k′(L) since k′(−) commutes with the W-action.
Now
suppose that k′(L)w = k′(L) There exists some n ∈Z invertible in k such that L is defined by a group
homomorphism
ρL : Y →µn(k)
where µn(k) is the group of n-th roots of unity in k. By assumption, we have that for all λ ∈Y,
φ(ρL(λ · w −λ)) = 1.
In other words, ρL(λ · w −λ) is in the kernel of the group homomorphism µn(k) →µn(k′). We claim
that µn(k) →µn(k′) is injective. Let ζ ∈µn(k) such that φ(ζ) = 1. Assume that ζ ̸= 1. Since k is a
domain and ζn −1 = 0, we must have that ζn−1 + ζn−2 + . . . + 1 = 0. We can then compute
0 = φ(ζn−1 + ζn−2 + . . . + 1) = φ(ζ)n−1 + φ(ζ)n−2 + . . . + φ(1) = n.
However, n is assumed to be invertible in k and hence also in k′, so this contradicts our assumption
that ζ ̸= 1. We conclude that ρL(λ · w −λ) = 1 which translates into the condition that Lw = L.
□
We return to the general case where k ∈{K, O, F} with ring homomorphism Z′ →k.
For each L ∈o, we can find some LO ∈Chµ(T, O) such that k(LO) ∼= L. Indeed, if k = F, this
follows from the existence of a Teichmüller lift F× →O×. For k = K, this can be constructed using
the fact that the roots of unity of K are contained in O. Define oO = LO · W for some L ∈o. We can
then set oK = K(LO) · W and oF = F(LO). Note that ok = o. For L ∈o, the sheaf LO is torsion, and
hence ok ⊆Chµ(T, k). By Lemma 7.6.1, extension of scalars k(−) : oO →ok gives a W-equivariant
bijection. We can then abuse notation and simply write o to refer to any ok with k ∈{K, O, F} under
these bijections. Likewise, for any L ∈o, we will simultaneously view it as an object in ok for any
such k. Moreover, extension of scalars defines a 2-functor
k(−) : \ParBS
/ (G, O, o) →\ParBS
/ (G, k, o).
Recall the 2-category \◦DBS
/ (hk, W, o) constructed in §5.7.11. The goal of this section is to construct
a 2-functor
Υk

/ (G, k, o).
The construction is analogous to the non-monodromic version constructed by Riche and Williamson
[RW18]. We will first define ΥO

are satisfied by extending scalars of ΥO

7.8. Monodromic Riche–Williamson Theorem.
Theorem 7.8.1. Let k ∈{K, O, F} and o ⊆Chµ(T, k) be a W-orbit. The 2-functor
Υk
RW : \
◦DBS
/ (hk, W, o) →\ParBS
/ (G, k, o)
is an equivalence of 2-categories.
Remark 7.8.2. We expect that it should be possible to construct Υk
RW when G is a Kac–Moody
group which extends the neutral block equivalence from [San25, Theorem 5.3.1]. The main problem is
that ICL
s for Ls ̸= L has no canonical representative and the Whittaker model is too large to rigidify
these choices.
Remark 7.8.3. Theorem 5.6.2 and Theorem 7.8.1 can be combined to give another proof of the
monodromic-endoscopic equivalence for the geometric Hecke categories of parity sheaves ([San25, The-
orem 5.11.1]).
Remark 7.8.4.
(1) While Theorem 7.8.1 is only stated for k ∈{K, O, F}, it is easy to deduce a
version for k = Qℓor k = Fℓ. In these cases, the torsion constraint on L ∈o ensures that we
can find some ℓ-modular triple (K, O, F) such that L (as well as any member of its W-orbit)
descends to an O-local system along either the morphism O →K →Qℓor O →F →Fℓ. From
here, one can apply extension-of-scalars to deduce the Qℓ- and Fℓ-variants from Theorem 7.8.1.
(2) Instead of taking o to be a W-orbit in Chµ(T, k), we could instead take o = Chµ(T, k). In
this generality, Theorem 7.8.1 still holds. This can be easily deduced from the decomposition
of \◦DBS
/ (hk, W, Chµ(T, k)) and \ParBS
/ (G, k, Chµ(T, k)) into sub-2-categories,
\
◦DBS
/ (hk, W, Chµ(T, k)) ∼=
G
W -orbits o⊆Chµ(T,k)
\
◦DBS
/ (hk, W, o),
\ParBS
/ (G, k, Chµ(T, k)) ∼=
G
W -orbits o⊆Chµ(T,k)
\ParBS
/ (G, k, o).
We will prove Theorem 7.8.1 using the same strategy invoked in [San25, Theorem 5.3.1]. It is clear
from its definition that Υk
RW is a bijection on 0- and 1-morphisms. Therefore, we only need to show
that the induced functor on morphism categories
Υk

/L′(hk, W, o) →L\ParBS

Define a category

By Theorem 5.5.4 and

˜Υk

Proposition 7.8.5. Let k ∈{K, O, F} and o ⊆Chµ(T, k) be a W-orbit. For all L, L′ ∈o, the functor

7.8.6. Reduction to Fields. We will prove that Υk

Claim 7.8.7. ˜ΥF

We delay the proof of Claim 7.8.7 until §7.8.8.
Proof of Proposition 7.8.5 assuming Claim 7.8.7. Let L, L′ ∈o and x, y ∈Exp(W) such that Lx =
L′ = Ly. Consider the morphism of free O-modules of finite rank
α : Hom L)◦DBS

Proof of Theorem 7.8.1 assuming Proposition 7.8.5.
