Appendix A. Three-Color Relations
This appendix serves to fill in the details needed to complete the proof of Claim 5.4.10. The goal is
to verify the three-color relations need to construct the functor
ΥA : \D/(W, h, o) →\ABS
/ (W, h, o).
We will first explain the general strategy. We assume that W is a rank 3 finite Coxeter group. Let
ΓL : BL
x →BL
y and ΓR : BL
x →BL
y denote the (linear combinations of) monodromic Elias–Williamson
graphs for the left and right sides of a 3-color relation, respectively. Note that by definition ΥA(ΓL)
and ΥA(ΓR) both take 1-tensors to 1-tensors. As a result, if the space of degree 0 homomorphisms
Hom \◦ABS
/ (BL
x , BL
y ) is a rank 1 k-module, then we are done. It turns out that this happens in almost
every case with the following exceptions:
(Case 1) In type A3, when W ◦
L = W, the degree 0 Hom space has rank 2.
(Case 2) In type B3, when W ◦
L = W, the degree 0 Hom space has rank rkk(h) + 68.
(Case 3) In type B3, when W ◦
L is type A3, the degree 0 Hom space has rank 3.
(Case 4) In type H3, when W ◦
L = W, the degree 0 Hom space has rank
rkk(h) + 3
4

+ 34
rkk(h) + 2
3

+ 578
rkk(h) + 1
2

+ 6644 rkk(h) + 71160.
Cases 1 and 2 follow from the well-definedness of the Soergel functor in the non-monodromic setting.
This was already proved using localization in [EW16]. Case 4 is omitted by Assumption 5.4.1. As a
result, the only case that requires a meaningful amount of work is Case 3.
In this section, we will first explain how we can enumerate all the possible labelings of ΓL and ΓR
by monodromy parameters. It is impossible to enumerate all the W-sets; however, we only have to
restrict our attention to the small subset which may appear on a given Elias–Williamson graph. Given
a monodromic Elias–Williamson graph Γ : BL
x →BL
y , the relations in the Hecke category only depend
on whether two adjacent faces are labelled the same along an edge. As a result, we will be able to
iterate over labelings of the faces of an Elias–Williamson graph where we only keep track of whether
faces are equal or not. This gives us a finite parameter space.
Next, we will use this parameter space to compute the rank of the degree 0 Hom spaces for types
A3, B3, and H3 using a case-by-case analysis on the parameter space and the defect formula (Corollary
4.5.3). Using the argument given earlier, this computation will show that ΥA(ΓL) = ΥA(ΓR) in all of
these cases except when W is of type B3 and W ◦
L is of type A3. We will then handle this case separately.
We will use the algebraic monodromic-endoscopic equivalence (Proposition 3.5.7) to handle this case.
Finally, in types A1×I2(m), since there are infinitely many cases, we are unable to give an exhaustion
of the parameter space. Instead, we will appeal to a Coxeter theoretic argument to show using the
defect formula that the degree 0 Hom spaces are rank 1.
The computations in this appendix crucially rely on computer assisted computation. The author
has implemented the necessary algorithms in Python and SageMath– the code can be found here:
(A.0.1)
https://github.com/colton5007/Monodromic-Three-Color-Relations
It includes the exhaustion of the monodromy parameter spaces and the defect calculations used to
compute the rank of the degree 0 Hom spaces.
A.1. Finding Possible Cases.
Definition A.1.1. Let Γ be an Elias–Williamson graph. Let F be a subset of the faces of Γ. A
F-partial monodromy labeling of an Elias–Williamson graph Γ is a surjective map
c : F ↠{1, . . . , n}
for some n ∈Z≥1 such that for all F ∈F and G1, G2 ∈F faces adjacent to F, if the edge separating
F and G1 and the edge separating F and G2 are both colored by some s ∈S, then c(G1) = c(G2).


===== PDF PAGE 90 =====

90
COLTON SANDVIK
Two F-partial monodromy labelings c, c′ : F →{1, . . . , n} are said to be equivalent if there exists
a permutation σ of {1, . . . , n} such that σ ◦c′ = c. We write |c| = n and say that n is the number of
parameters for c. If F is the set of all faces of Γ, then we say that c is a complete monodromy labeling.
Of course, any monodromic Elias–Williamson graph automatically comes equipped with a complete
monodromy labeling. It is not always the case that a complete monodromy labeling arises via a group
action W on {1, . . . , n}. Nonetheless, a complete monodromy labeling of an Elias–Williamson graph
is enough to determine the monodromic subexpressions of the domain and codomain.
In particu-
lar, a complete monodromy labeling is sufficient for computing right-hand side of the defect formula
(Corollary 4.5.3) even if the left-hand side is not well-defined.
The following algorithm provides a means of computing the parameter space of complete monodromy
labelings.
Algorithm A.1.2. Let Γ be an Elias–Williamson graph. Let F1, . . . , Fn be an enumeration of the
faces of Γ. Define Fi = {F1, . . . , Fi} for each 1 ≤i ̸= n.
(1) There is a unique F1-partial monodromy labeling c1 taking F1 to 1. Define P1 = {c1}.
(2) Recursively, assume we have defined the set Pi of Fi-partial monodromy labelings. For each
c ∈Pi and 1 ≤j ≤|c| + 1, we can attempt to define a Fi+1-partial monodromy labeling c′ by
extending c on Fi and labeling Fi+1 by j.
We will then check that c′ is a valid partial monodromy labeling as follows. We iterate
through each 1 ≤k ≤i + 1 and each pair of adjacent faces F, F ′ of Fk such that the edges
separating Fk from F and F ′ are both colored by s. If F, F ′ ∈Fi+1 and c′(F) ̸= c′(F ′), then c′
is not a valid partial monodromy labeling and can be discarded from consideration. Otherwise,
continue until all such k and pairs (F, F ′) are checked. If the process completes without c′
being rendered invalid, then c′ is a valid Fi+1-partial monodromy labeling.
We then define a list Pi+1 consisting of the Fi+1-partial monodromy labelings produced as
above.
(3) After n iterations, the above procedure produces a set Pn consisting of complete monodromy
labelings.
It is not hard to check that every complete monodromy labeling (up to equivalence) arises via
Algorithm A.1.2. We keep the notation from the above. Given a complete monodromy labeling c :
{F1, . . . , Fn} ↠{1, . . . , k}, we can replace c by an equivalent complete monodromy labeling such that
c(Fi) ≤max1≤j≤i−1(c(Fj) + 1). Let ci be the Fi-partial monodromy labeling obtained by restricting
c to Fi. It is clear that F1 ∈P1. Arguing by induction, if ci ∈Pi, then our condition that c(Fi) ≤
max1≤j≤i−1(c(Fj) + 1) ensures that ci+1 ∈Pi+1.
Remark A.1.3. Algorithm A.1.2 is not very optimized. It is quick enough for the purposes of the
present work, but grows quite quickly as the number of faces and size of W increases. The algorithm’s
efficiency also highly depends on choice of face enumeration. When choosing an enumeration at the
(i + 1)-st step, it is best to choose a face adjacent to the first i faces with maximal valence.
A.2. Types A3, B3, and H3. While Algorithm A.1.2 is sufficient for labeling one diagram, we actually
need to label two diagrams simultaneously, both ΓL and ΓR. To handle this, we will simply apply the
algorithm to the horizontal concatenation Γ′
R ⋆ΓL where Γ′
R is the horizontal reflection of ΓR.
(1) In type A3, there are 30 complete monodromy labelings of ΓL and ΓR.
(2) In type B3, there are 98 complete monodromy labelings of ΓL and ΓR.
(3) In type H3, there are 164 complete monodromy labelings of ΓL and ΓR.
As discussed before, one can compute the graded ranks of the Hom spaces in which ΓL and ΓR reside
using Corollary 4.5.3 and the code provided in (A.0.1).
All these graded ranks are 1 except in 3
cases: the non-monodromic cases in type A3 and B3 and the monodromic case in type B3 where
the endoscopic group has type A3. When the graded rank of the Hom space is 1, then the 1-tensor
argument from before is enough to show that ΥA(ΓL) = ΥA(ΓR). The non-monodromic cases in type

A.3. Type A1 × I2(m). Any reflection subgroup of a product of Coxeter groups can be written as a
product of reflection subgroups of the individual factors. As a result, the possible reflection subgroups
of the Coxeter group of type A1 × I2(m) are of the following types: A1 × I2(v) and I2(v) where
0 ≤v ≤m divides m (by definition, we take I2(0) = 1 and I2(1) = A1). Since ΥA(ΓL) and ΥA(ΓR)
both take 1-tensor to 1-tensors, the desired relation will follow from the following lemma.
Lemma A.3.1. Write S = {s, t, u} where ms,t = m, ms,u = 2, and mt,u = 2. The k-module of degree
0 homomorphisms
Hom \◦ABS
/ (BL
smu, BL
utm)
is rank 1.
Proof. Let w0 denote the maximal element in W. By Corollary 4.5.3, it suffices to show that for
all proper (i.e., not all 1’s) L-monodromic subexpressions e of smu and f of utm that d(smu, e) +
d(utm, f) > 0. We will show something even stronger, namely, that
d(smu, e) > 0
and
d(utm, f) > 0.


===== PDF PAGE 94 =====

94
COLTON SANDVIK
We will just show the first inequality as the other follows from a similar argument.
Note that
dec1(smu, e) = decm+1(utm, f) and can only be equal to U1 or U0. Let e′ = e≥2 which is a subex-
pression of sm. A simple computation for dihedral groups shows that d(sm, e′) ≥0 with equality if
and only if e′ is the all 1’s expression. We are then done unless e′ is the all 1’s subexpression. Since
we are assuming that e is proper, this means that e1 = U0 which implies that d(smu, e) > 0.
□
Appendix B. Mixed Derived Categories
Achar–Riche–Vay introduced a mixed derived category associated to the Elias–Williamson diagram-
matic Hecke category [ARV20]. Their mixed derived category was later used in [RV24] to extend the
Koszul duality equivalence of [AMRW19] to arbitrary Coxeter groups. One of the main reasons for
studying the mixed derived category is that it comes equipped with a recollement formalism. This
gives rise to a theory of standard and costandard objects which generate the mixed derived category.
Moreover, the Hom spaces between standards and costandards are simple to compute.
In fact, most of the constructions in [ARV20] hold in larger generality. Namely, the key properties
used are about the double leaves basis. We can then generalize their construction to produce a mixed
derived category for any object-adapted cellular category.
The principal goal of this section is to
develop this theory with two key examples in mind: (1) the category of monodromic Bott–Samelson
bimodules and (2) the monodromic diagrammatic Hecke category. We omit many of the proofs in this
section and refer to [ARV20] for detailed arguments.
B.1. Object-Adapted Cellular Categories. Object-adapted cellular categories were first intro-
duced in [EL16]. A gentle treatment of the subject can be found in [EMTW20] whose terminology we
closely follow.
Let k be a noetherian domain of finite global dimension. Let R be a graded commutative ring such
that R0 ∼= k.
Definition B.1.1. An object-adapted cellular category6 (abbv. OACC) is a graded R-linear category
C equipped with the following data:
• There is a set Λ of objects of C and a partial order ≤on Λ.
• There is an R-linear contravariant involution D on C.
• For each X ∈C and each λ ∈Λ, there are two finite families
(LLT )T ∈E(X,λ) ⊂HomC(X, λ)
and
(LLS)S∈E(λ,X) ⊂HomC(λ, X)
of homogenous morphisms, indexed by finite sets E(X, λ) and E(λ, X).
• For each object X ∈C and λ ∈Λ, there are pairwise inverse maps between the indexing sets
E(X, λ) and E(λ, X) that we denote by · in either direction.
This data is subject to the following requirements:
(CC1) Define LLλ
S,T := LLS ◦LLT ∈HomC(X, Y ) for T ∈E(X, λ) and S ∈E(λ, Y ). Consider the
family
LLλ(X, Y ) := {LLλ
S,T : T ∈E(X, λ), S ∈E(λ, Y )} ⊆HomC(X, Y ).
Then the family LL(X, Y ) := S
λ∈Λ LLλ(X, Y ) is a graded R-basis of HomC(X, Y ).
(CC2) For all T ∈E(X, λ), we have that D(LLT ) = LLT .
(CC3) For each λ ∈Λ, the sets E(λ, λ) and E(λ, λ) are just singletons {∗} and
LL∗= idλ = LL∗.
(CC4) For each λ ∈Λ, the collection of morphisms
Hom≤λ(X, Y ) := spanR

[
µ≤λ
LLµ(X, Y )

⊂HomC(X, Y )
6In [EL16], these are called strictly object-adapted cellular categories.


===== PDF PAGE 95 =====

SOERGEL CALCULUS FOR MONODROMIC HECKE CATEGORIES
95
forms a two-sided ideal C≤λ in C, i.e., it is stable under pre- and post-composition with arbitrary
morphisms in C.
Definition B.1.2. Let C be an object-adapted cellular category. A map π : Ob(C) →Λ is called an
evaluation if
(1) λ ≤π(X) whenever E(X, λ) ̸= ∅,
(2) π(λ) = λ for all λ ∈Λ.
An object X ∈C is said to be reduced with respect to π if
(1) E(X, π(X)) is a singleton {∗},
(2) the coefficient of idπ(X) in LL∗◦LL∗is 1.
Remark B.1.3. Let C be an object-adapted cellular category with evaluation π.
(1) For all λ ∈Λ, by (CC3), we have that λ is reduced.
(2) If X, Y are reduced and π(X) = λ = π(Y ), then there is a unique map LLλ : X →Y in
LLλ(X, Y ).
B.2. Homological Algebra Preliminaries. Let C be a graded R-linear category. We can define a
category C◦whose objects are symbols X(n) where X ∈Ob(C) and n ∈Z, and whose morphisms are

The category C◦admits a natural autoequivalence (1) : C◦→C◦defined by X(n) 7→X(n + 1). For
j ∈Z≥0, we can define (j) as the j-th power of (1) whose inverse is denoted by (−j). We define C◦,⊕
as the additive hull of C◦. It inherits a grading shift autoequivalence (n) for each n ∈Z from C◦.
We can view R as a graded R-linear category itself with one object ∗. Observe that R◦is a monoidal
category with ∗(n) ⊗∗(m) = ∗(n + m). Note that the additive hull of R◦is equivalent to the monoidal
category of finitely generated graded free R-modules Freefg,Z(R).
Since C is enriched over graded
R-modules, the category C◦(resp. C◦,⊕) is naturally an R◦-module (resp. Freefg,Z(R)-module). In

B.3. Categories Attached to Locally Closed Subsets.


===== PDF PAGE 96 =====

96
COLTON SANDVIK
B.3.1. Topology on Λ. Let (Λ, ≤) be a poset. We say that a subset I ⊆Λ is closed if for any λ, µ ∈Λ
with µ ∈I and λ ≤µ, then λ ∈I. A subset J ⊆Λ is called open if Λ \ J is closed. Given any subset
K ⊆Λ, we can consider its closure
K := {λ ∈Λ | ∃µ ∈K such that λ ≤µ},
which is the smallest closed set containing K. Finally, we say that K ⊆X is locally closed if K is
open in K, or equivalently if K is closed in Λ \ (K \ K).
Let λ ∈Λ, we will write
{≤λ} := {µ ∈Λ | µ ≤λ}
and
{< λ} := {µ ∈Λ | µ < λ}.
B.3.2. Locally Closed Subsets. Let C be an OACC with evaluation π and fix a closed subset I ⊆Λ.

for the additive hull of C◦

B.3.5. Case of a Singleton. We consider the special case when I = {λ} for some λ ∈Λ.
Lemma B.3.6. Let X ∈C be reduced and π(X) = λ. By definition E(X, λ) = {∗} is a singleton. The
image of the canonical map LL∗: X →λ in C◦,⊕
{λ} is an isomorphism.
Proof. Since X is reduced, we must have that LL∗◦LL∗= idλ in C◦,⊕
{λ}. On the other hand, by Lemma
B.3.4, we have an isomorphism EndC◦,⊕
{λ}(X) ∼= R0. As a result, idX = αLLλ
∗,∗for some α ∈R0. By
applying LL∗◦(−) to both sides, we get that LL∗= α LL∗. Therefore, α = 1.
□
By Lemma B.3.6, for any reduced objects X, Y ∈C with π(X) = λ = π(Y ), there is a canonical
isomorphism X

B.3.8. Open and Closed Inclusions. Let I ⊆Λ be a locally closed subset. Write I = I0 \ I1 for some

Let I ⊆Λ be a finite locally closed subset. If λ ∈I, then we will simplify the notation for the

B.5. Standards and Costandards.
B.5.1. Generation.
Definition B.5.2. The mixed derived category of C is the bounded homotopy category
Dm(C) := KbC◦,⊕.
As in Remark B.3.3, C◦,⊕is not generally equivalent to C◦,⊕
Λ
. The situation is somewhat rectified
after passing to mixed derived categories provided the following generation assumption holds.
Assumption B.5.3. Every object X ∈C when viewed as an object in Dm(C) is isomorphic to an
object in Dm
I (C) for some finite locally closed subset I ⊆Λ.
Whenever Assumption B.5.3 holds, the canonical embedding
Dm
Λ (C) ,→Dm(C)
is an equivalence of categories. Indeed, the assumption implies that Dm(C) is generated as a triangu-
lated category by reduced objects in C. Therefore, the embedding is essentially surjective.
Remark B.5.4. Assumption B.5.3 is fairly mild. For example, this is known to hold whenever k is a
complete local ring or a field by [EL16, Proposition 2.24]. Additionally, it holds without any restriction
on k when C is the Hecke category of a Coxeter group [ARV20, Lemma 6.2]; although, their proof
crucially uses properties of the Hecke category which are not featured in a general OACC. It would
be interesting to reinterpret Assumption B.5.3 as a condition on the cellular structure. This could
lead to interesting generalizations of categorification results that historically have been beholden to
constraints on k (see Proposition B.6.2).
B.5.5. Definitions of Standards and Costandards. Let I ⊆Λ be a finite locally closed subset. For each
λ ∈I ⊆Λ, we define the standard and costandard objects in Dm

and

When I = Λ, we will sometimes omit the superscript in the notation.

and other basic properties of pushforwards/pullbacks along locally closed subsets.
